# DECISIONS — Race Through Time
Newest last. Owner = Luke. A Claude working choice is reversible and listed so it can be overturned.

## Recorded
- **DEC-001 · 27 Sep 2026 · Owner (kickoff instruction).** Keep Market Marathon unchanged. RTT launches English-first at one long-form release a day for 28 public days, then the one-vs-two A–B–B–A test. Production capacity is separate from release cadence; build an approved buffer, no weekly quota. No public publishing, activated schedules, purchases, credential exposure, permission changes or overwrites without explicit owner authorisation. Uploads stay private until Luke has watched and approved the exact final render; an agent-editable flag is not approval.
- **DEC-002 · 27 Sep 2026 · Claude working choice.** RTT renderer changes go in a copied player file plus RTT config inside an RTT kit. C2-2 files are never edited for RTT.
- **DEC-003 · 27 Sep 2026 · Claude research rule.** Jolpica/Ergast F1 data is not a production source: its data licence is CC BY-NC-SA 4.0 (non-commercial), verified from its TERMS.md. Revisit only with written commercial permission.
- **DEC-004 · 27 Sep 2026 · Claude working choice.** RTT-002 is the proposed first dataset and pilot subject (subject to D-02 and the IQ-02 rights check).

- **DEC-005 · 27 Sep 2026 · Owner (D-01).** The RTT YouTube channel exists: handle `@racethroughtime` (corrected by Luke 27 Sep 2026; first given with a typo), on Google account luke@marketmarathon.com, the same account as Market Marathon. Channel ID to be read from Studio at IQ-07. Consequence: RTT gets its own YouTube authorisation (refresh token) and its uploader must refuse to run unless the authorised channel ID is RTT's.
- **DEC-006 · 27 Sep 2026 · Owner (D-02).** RTT lives in a separate repo. Created by Claude via Luke's Chrome on his instruction: `marketmarathon/race-through-time`, **public**, empty (read back: HTTP 200, no refs). Public because standard Actions runners are free on public repos; private repos get a limited monthly allowance (verified on GitHub's 2026 pricing page).
  Public-repo rules: commit only code, configs, contracts and open-licence datasets with attribution. Never commit licensed data, music, logos/images without redistribution rights, credentials or unreleased masters. Renders upload straight to YouTube as private; no GitHub Releases for unreleased films; keep workflow artifacts to the shortest retention (artifact visibility on public repos to be confirmed at setup). Licensed audio is pulled at render time from private storage, as Market Marathon already does.
- **DEC-007 · 27 Sep 2026 · Owner (D-03).** Research method for RTT: Claude builds datasets directly from primary or open-licence sources with scripted checks. The independent check is ChatGPT deep research, run by Luke, reconstructing decisive events blind (without Claude's results). Market Marathon keeps its own rule.
- **DEC-008 · 27 Sep 2026 · Claude research finding (RTT-002).** Formula 1's published guidelines state its statistics, results and timing data are protected by copyright and database rights; individual items may be used incidentally in editorial material, but substantial reuse or commercial scraping is not permitted. Jolpica is non-commercial (DEC-003). Working route: build from a CC BY-SA 4.0 source (Wikipedia) with attribution; official site used only to eyeball individual decisive results, never scraped.

- **DEC-009 · 28 Sep 2026 · Owner (settings; environment created by Luke, allowlist set in his Chrome on his instruction).** claude.ai Settings → Capabilities → Additional allowed domains: `*.wikipedia.org`, `*.wikimedia.org`, `*.wikidata.org` (Domain allowlist stays "Package managers only"; "All domains" deliberately not used). Claude Code cloud environment **"Race Through Time"** created: Custom network = default package-manager list + the same three domains; no variables or setup script. `Default` left unchanged for igaming work.
- **DEC-010 · 28 Sep 2026 · Claude working choice (tooling).** Cowork sessions cannot push to race-through-time (GitHub proxy: repo not in the session's authorised set; no attach option in Cowork). Until that changes: repo writes from Cowork go through GitHub's web upload in Luke's Chrome, only within an owner-authorised scope, and every commit is read back byte-for-byte. Code work goes to Claude Code cloud sessions (environment "Race Through Time"), which push a session branch and open a pull request for Luke to merge.
- **DEC-011 · 28 Sep 2026 · Claude research method (RTT-002).** Wikipedia is read through the MediaWiki API in Luke's Chrome when the cloud IP is rate-limited (HTTP 429). Exact revision IDs and retrieval times are recorded; every transferred file is verified by SHA-256; driver identity = Wikipedia page ID after resolving redirects. Independent-check files are validated and hash-frozen in `state/` before any build is compared against them.

- **DEC-012 · 28 Sep 2026 · Owner (D-04).** RTT-002 follows the official Formula 1 treatment. Evidence (formula1.com, read not scraped): the Indianapolis 500 counted towards the World Championship 1950–1960, and F1 treats those wins as World Championship Grand Prix wins — e.g. "Troy Ruttman … won just once, in 1952, to become the youngest World Championship Grand Prix winner" (F1's "one-win wonders" article, 4 Apr 2020); Sam Hanks's 1957 Indy win is listed among the oldest F1 Grand Prix winners (article of 18 Jun 2026). So the 11 Indy 500 wins stay in. Shared drives: one win to each credited driver, as in F1's race results. Tie order (whoever reached the total first ranks higher) is a display rule, not data; there is no official rule for it.
- **DEC-013 · 28 Sep 2026 · Owner (D-06).** "Carlos Sainz" in the independent check is Wikipedia's "Carlos Sainz Jr.": the only driver of that name among the 116 winners in either source, with the same four wins on the same dates (2022 British, 2023 Singapore, 2024 Australian, 2024 Mexico City GPs).
- **DEC-014 · 28 Sep 2026 · Owner.** Branch `rtt-002-data` merged into `main` (pull request #1, merge commit `7cde5df`). The branch is kept for the record.

- **DEC-015 · 28 Sep 2026 · Owner (D-07a).** Accept the RTT-002 top-20 career totals as checked without a full official F1/FIA table: Wikipedia season pages, Wikipedia's list of winners, the F1-archive reconstruction (independent check) and StatsF1 agree for every driver; official totals seen for Hamilton, Schumacher and Prost all match. With D-07(b) passed, **RTT-002 passes DATA_AUDIT**. Next: IQ-04 (RTT player) in a Claude Code cloud session.

- **DEC-016 · 28 Sep 2026 · Claude working choice (IQ-04, player structure).** The RTT player lives in `kits/rtt-002/`: `player_rtt.html` (drawing, copied from C2-2's `player_formatc2.html`), `rtt_timeline.js` (event, tie and pacing rules, shared by page and driver), `rtt.js` (driver, copied from `formatc.js`, same environment contract plus optional `RTT_CONFIG`) and `config.json` (everything RTT-specific). Copied from `marketmarathon/bars` @ `2a10877` (full hash `2a10877695e83683c3caae651cad61fc686614eb`), kit `MarketMarathon_RaceKit_RF_US_C2_v3.zip`; nothing in bars was changed. Archivo comes from the npm package `@fontsource/archivo` 5.3.0 (SIL OFL 1.1, licence file read in the package) rather than the TTFs in the Market Marathon kit, whose licence file is not in the kit.
- **DEC-017 · 28 Sep 2026 · Claude working choice (IQ-04, step at event).** A count changes on the first frame of its race and holds until the next race; no in-between value is ever drawn. Only the bar's length glides (0.35 s, `glide.bar_sec`) and its row glides with C2-2's 0.55 s eased rank glide. Consequence kept from C2-2: a driver who jumps into the top ten from far below slides up from under the board and is fully faded for up to 22 frames (0.73 s) in the 1984–89 slice, so their label appears then, correct from its first visible frame. Event start times are whole frames, so tests can land on them exactly.
- **DEC-018 · 28 Sep 2026 · Claude working choice (IQ-04, pacing placeholder).** 0.5 s per race; ×1.4 when the visible top-ten order or membership changes, ×1.0 when a visible bar grows, ×0.8 when nothing visible changes or after more than three visible-but-unchanged races in a row. The player refuses any multiplier outside 0.8–1.4. Full RTT-002 run = 16,062 frames at 30 fps (8 min 55 s including 3 s intro and 8 s closing card). IQ-05 sets the real pace.
- **DEC-019 · 28 Sep 2026 · Claude working choice (IQ-04, presentation defaults).** Colours: a 12-colour palette cycled in `drivers.csv` order (no team or nationality colours: not in the data, and team marks are excluded by the contract), so colours repeat. Time label bottom-right of the board: season at 112 px, Grand Prix, then date written "13 November 1988"; during a passage's lead-in it shows the last race before the passage. Value labels "1 win" / "N wins". Footer: "Race Through Time · Data derived from Wikipedia (CC BY-SA 4.0)". No logo (none exists for RTT).
- **DEC-020 · 28 Sep 2026 · Claude working choice (IQ-04, tests).** Player tests check the text the player draws on every race frame (a draw-call log in the page) against counts computed independently from `win_credits.csv`, and read value labels back from the pixels of the first frame of every race with tesseract OCR. Test frames and phone stills stay in `tests/output/` (gitignored) and are not committed or uploaded; only the written results (`tests/player/RESULTS.md`) are.

- **DEC-021 · 28 Sep 2026 · Owner.** Luke granted the Claude GitHub App write access to `marketmarathon/race-through-time` (the app's repository access is now `igaming-engine` and `race-through-time` only). Claude Code cloud sessions can now push branches and open pull requests here; this replaces the GitHub web-upload workaround of DEC-010 for Claude Code sessions. Cowork sessions still cannot push.
- **DEC-022 · 28 Sep 2026 · Owner decisions for IQ-05 (Claude's recommendations, accepted by Luke).** (1) Pilot passage 2014–2021: 160 races; Hamilton goes from 22 to 103 wins, equals Schumacher's 91 at the 2020 Eifel GP and passes him at the 2020 Portuguese GP. (2) No two drivers visible at the same time share a colour. (3) Enlarge the axis numbers, the date and the footer until they are readable at phone size. (4) Keep the slide-in for drivers entering the top ten, but shorten the time they are hidden after their win. (5) Render the current pace (0.5 s per race) plus one faster variant for side-by-side comparison. (6) Text only, no logo.

- **DEC-023 · 28 Sep 2026 · Claude working choice (IQ-05, colours).** Each driver gets one colour for the whole RTT-002 video, assigned by `rtt_timeline.js` `assignColours()` over the whole race file (never the passage window), so a pilot and the full video agree. "On screen together" = the top ten after a race plus the top ten after each of the 3 races before it (`colour_rule.fade_races`), because a row that drops out takes up to about 23 frames to fade. Such drivers never share a colour, and their colours must also be at least CIEDE2000 18 apart as drawn (`colour_rule.min_delta_e`), so near-copies do not count as "different". Drivers are coloured in the order they first reach the top ten, each taking the first palette colour that fits; the player refuses to render if the palette runs out, or if the pace is so fast that 3 races cannot cover the fade. Result: 40 drivers ever reach the top ten and at most 12 can be on screen together, so 12 colours are needed. IQ-04's 12 were not clearly distinct once drawn (closest pair CIEDE2000 9.6: three purples, two greens, two blues), and no bigger palette can help the moment when 12 are on screen, so the palette was replaced by 12 colours chosen from a grid of candidates to be as far apart as possible (closest pair CIEDE2000 18.5, about twice IQ-04's) while every bar keeps white-name contrast of at least 3.2:1 (IQ-04's weakest was 3.21). C2-2's first colour `#2E86DE` was kept; vivid colours are listed first so the earthier last four (mustard, forest green, brown, khaki) are used only when needed. The formula was checked against the published CIEDE2000 reference pairs. No team or nationality colours.
- **DEC-024 · 28 Sep 2026 · Claude working choice (IQ-05, phone sizes).** Axis numbers 20 → 34 px, date 28 → 34 px, footer 17 → 26 px on the 1920 frame (config `sizes`; without the key the player keeps C2-2's sizes). On a phone showing the video 390 pt wide that is 6.9 pt for the axis and date (driver names are 6.5 pt) and 5.3 pt for the footer. Nothing else on the board was moved or enlarged. `tests/player/phone_check.js` now reports PASS/FAIL against these thresholds on three stills (1988, the final board, and the pilot's 2020 Portuguese GP).
- **DEC-025 · 28 Sep 2026 · Claude working choice (IQ-05, drivers entering the top ten).** The slide-in is kept, but a driver joining the board now starts its slide just under the last row (instead of from wherever it was waiting below the board) and glides in with a 0.1 s time constant (`glide.entry_sec`) rather than the 0.55 s rank glide. "Clearly visible" in the test = row opacity at least 0.8 with its name and value label drawn; the rule is that this happens within 8 frames of the first frame of the race (frame +0 to +7). All other movement keeps C2-2's glide.
- **DEC-026 · 28 Sep 2026 · Claude working choice (IQ-05, pilot configs).** Pilot configs start from `config.json` through a new `"extends"` key (objects merge key by key), so colours, sizes and look cannot drift from the full video. Both pilots: window 2014-01-01 to 2021-12-31 (160 races; the board opens on the pre-2014 totals), normal title card with the range "2014 – 2021", 3 s hold on the final board (`pacing.end_hold_sec`) and no closing card (`outro_sec` 0; the player now skips the card when `outro_sec` is 0). Adaptive pacing (0.8–1.4×) unchanged.

- **DEC-027 · 28 Sep 2026 · Owner (Luke's feedback on the IQ-05 round-1 pilot).** (1) The pace of 0.5 s per race is fine; drop the fast variant. (2) He wants to see what is happening outside the top ten and asked for a top 20. (3) He wants the winner's bar to flash when they win. Round 2 (prompt `prompts/CODE_SESSION_IQ-05b.md`) builds two 2014–2021 pilots at 0.5 s per race that differ only in the board, A top 20 and B top ten plus a winner line, because blueprint section 9 / Playbook section 10 ask for the baseline against one controlled alternative at identical timestamps; and it turns the "flash" into one gentle highlight, because blueprint section 10 rules out flashes and strobing.
- **DEC-028 · 28 Sep 2026 · Claude working choice (IQ-05b, the two pilots).** Shared settings live in `config_pilot_2014_2021_base.json` (the round-1 `config_pilot_2014_2021.json`, renamed so it is not mistaken for a variant; the fast config is deleted); A (`config_pilot_2014_2021_top20.json`) and B (`config_pilot_2014_2021_top10_winner.json`) extend it and change only the board. The beat of each race is judged on the top ten in both (`pacing.judge_rows` 10); otherwise the top-20 board, which sees more position changes, would run slower and the two could not be compared at identical timestamps. Both are 2,472 frames = 82.4 s (FRAME_COUNT_ONLY).
- **DEC-029 · 28 Sep 2026 · Claude working choice (IQ-05b, top-20 colours).** On a 20-row board up to 22 drivers can be on screen together (20 rows plus rows still fading out; 59 drivers ever reach the top 20), so 22 colours are needed, against 12 for the top ten. The DEC-023 rule is not weakened: A has its own 22-colour palette, every pair at least CIEDE2000 18.2 apart as drawn (rule 18) with white-name contrast at least 3.2:1, found by a search over sRGB with those constraints; keeping the 12 top-ten colours and adding 10 could reach only 14.6. Costs: a driver's colour in A differs from B and from the full video (e.g. Hamilton is bright blue `#2E86DE` in B and the full video but dark teal `#1E6C84` in A; Verstappen orange in B, dark green in A), and to fit 22 the palette includes a very bright magenta and muted colours (olive, taupe, grey-green, grey), used last. The top-ten palette and every top-ten colour are unchanged.
- **DEC-030 · 28 Sep 2026 · Claude working choice (IQ-05b, winner highlight).** On the first frame of a race each credited winner whose bar is in the top N after the race is brightened 30% towards white with a soft 16 px glow, fading out (eased) over 0.4 s. A winner off the board gets nothing. A driver who also wins the next race stays lit through the streak instead of fading and re-lighting, so a repeat winner never flickers; the cost is that Hamilton's bar glows continuously through his winning runs. With one winner per race and beats of at least 0.4 s there are at most 3 onsets in any second (the tests measure it on every frame). Both pilots have it on; `highlight.enabled` false switches it off.
- **DEC-031 · 28 Sep 2026 · Claude working choice (IQ-05b, record moments).** The player derives the record events from the counts it already has (same rule as `scripts/build_rtt002_dataset.py`), and a test checks them against `data/rtt-002/record_progression.csv` on all 118 rows, so no new input file is needed. With `record_hold.enabled` a BECOMES_JOINT or BECOMES_SOLE race is held 1.5 s (EXTENDS_SOLE is not); "settled" = every row within 0.1 row of its place on the last frame of the hold (measured: Eifel 0.000; Portuguese 0.065 row, about 3 px in A and 6 px in B, so the glide is almost but not completely still when the next race lands; a 2.0 s hold would bring it to about 0.026). In 2014–2021 this fires twice: 11 Oct 2020 Eifel GP (Hamilton 91, joint record with Schumacher) and 25 Oct 2020 Portuguese GP (Hamilton 92, sole record). Every other beat stays within 0.8–1.4 × 0.5 s.
- **DEC-032 · 28 Sep 2026 · Claude working choice (IQ-05b, opening and ending).** No title card (`intro_sec` 0): frame 0 is the board with the title, and the subtitle carries the range ("… race by race, 2014 – 2021"); the opening board (totals before the 2014 opener, time label on the 2013 Brazilian GP) stays 2.0 s before the first race. The final board is on screen exactly 10 s from the first frame of the last race (`pacing.final_board_sec`; blueprint 8–12 s), no closing card. `config.json` keeps its title card, 3 s end hold and closing card.
- **DEC-033 · 28 Sep 2026 · Claude working choice (IQ-05b, board sizes, winner line, digits).** A: bars 6 px apart (C2-2: 22) and name and value text up to 0.78 of the bar height with namescale 1.0, the largest that still sits inside a bar: names and values 29 px (5.9 pt on a 390 pt phone) against 32 px names (6.5 pt) and 35 px values in the top ten. B: the winner line is 34 px, as large as the date, right-aligned under it; the season, Grand Prix and date move up 42 px. The line lists every credited driver with the career total the data gives after the race ("Won by Lewis Hamilton · 92 wins"; "… · 1 win"; shared drives "Won by A · n wins & B · m wins"). Digits: Archivo has tabular figures (`tnum`, every weight), but Chrome's canvas cannot switch OpenType features on, so every number is drawn on the widest digit's width as C2-2 did, now checked on every label by the tests.

- **DEC-036 · 28 Sep 2026 · Owner (process rule).** Luke: nationality should have been captured from the outset. **Rule: every future metric contract must list, before the data build, every attribute the video will display (for example nationality for flags) and its source, so the data build captures it.** RTT-002's contract now has a "Display attributes" section recording that nationality was added late (28 Sep 2026) and how it is sourced.
- **DEC-037 · 28 Sep 2026 · Claude working choice (IQ-05c, flags).** Flags sit in a narrow column just left of the bars (x 64–109), not at the start of each bar: on the plain navy ground every flag keeps its own colours (a red, white or blue flag on a bar of a similar colour would lose its edge), all flags line up in one column that the eye can run down, and no bar loses length or has its name pushed along. Each flag moves and fades with its row, has a thin light outline (for flags with white edges such as Finland's) and 3 px rounded corners. Size: 90% of the bar height, 4:3 as flag-icons draws them: 45 × 34 px on the 1920 frame = 9.1 × 6.9 pt on a phone showing the video 390 pt wide (27 × 21 device pixels); on the phone stills the UK, German, French, Brazilian, Spanish, Austrian, Argentine, Finnish and Australian flags are told apart, the Austrian flag being the smallest detail that still reads. Names are always drawn; a driver without a flag code gets no flag. The column's space is kept when flags are off (`flags.column`), so switching them off does not move anything. Flag files: npm `flag-icons` 7.5.0 (MIT, licence read, `reference/rights_ledger.md`); none committed.
- **DEC-038 · 28 Sep 2026 · Claude working choice (IQ-05c, wider bars).** `barend` 1150 → 1420 in the pilot: the leader's bar now ends at x 1337, about 70% of the frame width (blueprint section 10: two-thirds to three-quarters), against x 1140 (59%) before. The furthest text on any pilot frame, the leader's "· Emilia Romagna GP", ends at x 1745, inside the 64 px right margin (the tests check every label on every frame). `config.json` keeps C2-2's geometry.
- **DEC-039 · 28 Sep 2026 · Claude working choice (IQ-05c, event label).** A winner whose bar is on the board shows "· <Grand Prix>" right after its value, in the lighter grey of the Grand Prix line (so the number stays the brightest thing on the row), same size as the value (29 px). "Grand Prix" is written "GP" wherever it appears (so "United States GP West"); a race without those words keeps its name (the Indianapolis 500). It appears on the first frame of the race, together with the winner highlight, stays through the race, and when the next race lands it fades over 0.3 s (a driver who wins again just shows the new Grand Prix). Both drivers of a shared drive carry it. A winner off the board gets nothing. Config `event_label` {enabled, fade_sec}.
- **DEC-040 · 28 Sep 2026 · Claude working choice (IQ-05c, time block).** The season, Grand Prix and date are drawn left-aligned at x 820 with the date on y 1012 (config `time_label` mode "block"), in the empty space right of the lower bars. The place was chosen by measuring every item on all 2,502 pilot frames: in the block's height band nothing reaches further right than x 768. The tests check on every frame that the block overlaps no bar, flag, name, value, event label, axis number, title or footer, and report the smallest gap. The full video (`config.json`) keeps the bottom-right corner, where long bars pass in front of the year as in C2-2.
- **DEC-041 · 28 Sep 2026 · Claude working choice (IQ-05c, colours).** The DEC-023 rule is unchanged (no two drivers on screen together share a colour or come closer than CIEDE2000 18). New in the pilot (`colour_rule.priority`): the 22-colour top-20 palette from round 2 is reordered into 7 bright, light colours (CIELAB L* and C* both at least 50 as drawn), 6 vivid darker ones, then 9 muted ones; the 40 drivers who ever reach the top ten choose first, most career wins at the freeze first, and the 7 bright colours are kept for them (others use them only when nothing else fits). Because a tightly packed palette can defeat a one-pass choice, the assignment is a deterministic depth-first search in that priority order. Result: Hamilton blue (the same blue as in the full video), Schumacher green, Verstappen orange, Vettel magenta, Prost red-pink, Senna amber, Alonso yellow-olive; not every top-ten driver can be bright, because up to 21 of them can be on a 20-row board at once and only 7 colours are both bright and light while keeping white names readable (3.2:1).
- **DEC-042 · 28 Sep 2026 · Claude working choice (IQ-05c, overlay boxes).** Logo box 124 × 124 px at x 1732, y 18 (top right, inside the 64 px margin, above the first row); car box 440 × 200 px at x 1416, y 750 (bottom right, about 2.2:1, the usual shape of a side-on car picture); each picture is fitted inside its box keeping its shape. The bottom edge of the car box is y 950, keeping it out of the bottom 130 px (12% of the height), taken as the strip YouTube's player controls can cover. That figure is Claude's estimate, not a published YouTube measurement. The footer and the time block's date line were already inside that strip before this round and were not moved (question for Luke). The tests use plain rectangles made at test time as stand-ins (square gold for the logo, 2.2:1 grey for the car) and check that nothing enters either box on any frame.
- **DEC-043 · 28 Sep 2026 · Claude working choice (IQ-05c, nationality data).** Claude Code's own requests to the Wikipedia and Wikidata APIs got HTTP 429 all session (17:59 to about 18:20 UTC; one small test request answered 200, every content request 429) and were not worked around. Luke then supplied the extraction (made in his Chrome by Claude in Cowork with `scripts/extract_nationality.browser.js`, same method as the season pages): a package with SHA-256 `7237669e…054b` holding `data/rtt-002/source/wikipedia_wikidata_nationality.psv` and `wikidata_countries.psv`; each file's SHA-256 was checked against his before use. `scripts/rtt002_nationality.py` builds `data/rtt-002/driver_nationality.csv` deterministically: Wikipedia flag = primary value, mapped to ISO 3166-1 alpha-3 with Luke's table (UK→GBR, GER→DEU, NED→NLD, SUI→CHE, MON→MCO, others unchanged), flag file = ISO alpha-2. Cross-check: Wikidata P1532 where the item has it (36 drivers), otherwise P27 (80); agreement = the primary country is among the values. Result: 113 agree, 3 disagree (Jim Clark, Phil Hill, Luigi Fagioli), exactly as Luke's own check; the CSV keeps the Wikipedia value and the three are questions for Luke. As Luke instructed, `data/rtt-002/source/README.md` gained a section for the two new source files; no other existing file in `data/rtt-002/` was changed.
- **DEC-044 · 28 Sep 2026 · Claude working choice (IQ-05c, tests).** Variant B's test case is removed with its config; the winner line stays covered by the shared-drive fixture cases. New checks: flags against the nationality file on every frame; event labels and values against the data on every frame; the time block against everything else on every frame; the overlay boxes against everything else on every frame; and the named drivers' colours. A new fictional fixture case (`shared_drive_round3`, a fictional nationality file with one driver NOT FOUND) runs every round-3 feature on the pilot's 20-row geometry. Tesseract 5.3.4 was installed in this container from Ubuntu's packages (the same version as round 2) so the OCR read-back still runs.
- **DEC-045 · 28 Sep 2026 · Owner (Luke's feedback on the IQ-05 round-3 pilot).** Luke called round 3 "absolutely perfect", with these changes; everything else stays as in round 3. (1) **Logo**: the full Race Through Time logo with its "RACE THROUGH TIME" wordmark, not just the emblem: Luke's banner logo (gold wordmark over a globe, 1983 × 793 px, 2.5 : 1) with its edges faded to transparent, supplied by Claude in Cowork as `local_assets/rtt_logo.png`; logo box to that shape, about 330 × 132 px, top-right corner at 1920 width. Definition: a driver has a start at a championship race when that season's Drivers' Championship table cell for that round shows a classified position (a number), Ret, NC or DSQ, including any part of a shared or swapped-car cell such as "2†/ Ret"; DNS, DNQ, DNPQ, WD, EX, DNA, DNP or an empty cell is not a start; brackets and the marks † ‡ * ^ ~ are stripped and the "Race: …; Sprint: …" annotation dropped first; empty-header columns are dropped; round columns map to `races.csv` by order (2026: the 15 completed rounds); the Indianapolis 500 of 1950–1960 counts (DEC-012); one start per driver per race; rows match drivers through Wikipedia page IDs. Win rate = wins / starts at that moment, a percentage with one decimal, rounded half up. The label goes on EVERY visible bar ("win"/"start" for 1), win count in bold, numbers stepping only at races; the winner glow, date line, logo and car boxes and footer stay. DEC-036 applied: starts and win rate were added to the contract's "Display attributes" before the build. - **DEC-061 · 29 Sep 2026 · Claude working choice (render on GitHub, on Luke's instruction).** New workflow `.github/workflows/render_pilot.yml` renders the pilot on a GitHub-hosted runner and saves it only as a release in the private repo. (1) **Resolution 3840 × 2160** (the master; practical: a 120-frame 4K test in the Claude Code container, with ffmpeg installed there for the test, encoded at about 2 fps with x264 slow CRF 4 as in the config, so the 2,502-frame pilot was estimated at 20–30 minutes on one runner; the first run took 4 min 49 s to render and 6 min 15 s in all; one process, no shards, so no hand-off between jobs is needed) plus a **1920 × 1080 viewing copy** (x264 CRF 18) that is easier to play on a phone. (2) **Nothing public:** on a public repo, workflow artifacts can be downloaded by anyone signed in to GitHub (GitHub's documentation: people with read access can download artifacts, and everyone has read access to a public repo; docs.github.com was blocked from the container on 29 Sep 2026, so this was not re-read that day), and run logs are public too. The workflow therefore uploads no artifact and uses no cache; the pictures are fetched with `RTT_PRIVATE_TOKEN` into the runner's temporary folder (never the checkout), both SHA-256 hashes are checked before the render (mismatch = stop), a missing overlay stops the run, the videos go straight to a private-repo release with `gh release create`, and a final step (always run) deletes pictures and videos from the runner (GitHub-hosted runners are also discarded after each job). The logs show only hashes, frame counts and the release link; the secret is passed as an environment variable and never echoed (GitHub also masks it). `permissions: contents: read` for the public repo's own token. (3) **Trigger:** a `workflow_dispatch` workflow can only be started by hand once it is on the default branch, so the workflow also runs on a push to branch `claude/cool-mccarthy-kf9mow` that changes the workflow file itself; that is how it was first run, before merging. After merging it runs by hand from the Actions tab (inputs: config, width). The player input's hash is checked against `dataset_hashes.txt` and the video's size and frame count with ffprobe before anything is saved. Release tag `rtt-002-pilot5-<commit>-run<n>`, marked pre-release, with the commit, config, frame count and all hashes in its notes.

- **DEC-063 · 29 Sep 2026 · Claude working choices (RTT-002 full film, on Luke's instruction after DEC-062).** (1) **Scope and freeze:** all 1,164 championship races, 1950 British GP (13 May 1950) to the **data freeze, the 2026 Azerbaijan GP (26 Sep 2026)**, the last race in `data/rtt-002/races.csv` (manifest `freeze_race`; 15 of the 2026 rounds). (2) **Look unchanged:** `kits/rtt-002/config_rtt002_film.json` *extends* the approved `config_pilot_2014_2021_top20.json` and changes only the window, the subtitle's range ("1950 – 2026"), the pacing and the captions; name size stays 29 px, the furthest label ends at x 1740.5 (as the pilot), colours are the whole-dataset assignment the pilot already used. The board opens empty, so the first race lands on frame 0 (no lead-in) and the date line names a race on every frame. (3) **Pacing (`pacing.mode` "smooth", `rtt_timeline.js`):** baseline 0.5 s per race; each race's IQ-04 multiplier (1.4 top-ten change / 1.0 winner on the board / 0.8 quiet) is averaged over neighbours (Gaussian, sigma 3 races), set to 1.4 from 2 races before to 3 after every emphasis race (the 30 story moments and all 40 entries into the top ten: 59 races), then slew-limited so neighbouring multipliers differ by at most 0.1 (the limiter only ever slows a beat). "0.8–1.4 times the baseline pace" is read as in DEC-018 and the pilot: a multiplier on the time per race, so the quietest stretches run at 0.4 s per race and the busiest at 0.7 s. (4) **Story moments** (`scripts/rtt002_story.py` → new file `data/rtt-002/story_moments.csv`, list in `reports/RTT-002_story_moments.md`), fixed rules, 30 moments: the first race; every change of all-time leader (8: leader = first on the board under the DEC-012 tie rule, so a BECOMES_JOINT never changes it); a record equalled when that driver's next record row is taking the lead (8); the first driver to 50 and to 100 wins; the day each driver of today's top ten entered the all-time top ten (10); the freeze. Captions use fixed wording with names, numbers and dates from the audited files only. Each moment race is held (first race 2.5 s, leader change 3.0 s, record equalled 3.0 s, milestone 2.5 s, top-ten entry 2.5 s; the pilot held its two record races 2.0 s) and its caption stays 4.5 s or until the next one; the freeze caption stays over the 10 s final table. Caption: up to three right-aligned text lines above the car box (bottom line y 752, x to 1856), first line 34 px bold white, others 30 px in the date line's grey, digits on fixed pitch, 0.25 s fade in and 0.4 s out; axis grid lines stop short of it as they do for the logo and car boxes; nothing comes nearer than 16 px. Top-ten holds were lengthened from 2.0 to 2.5 s after the tests found a row 0.108 rows from its place at the end of Jim Clark's 2.0 s hold (limit 0.1, as the pilot). (5) **Running time:** 20,033 frames at 30 fps = **667.8 s (11 min 8 s)**, of which 10 s is the final table; not padded (no title card, no closing card). (6) **Tests:** new case `rtt002_film` (checks 29–32 in `tests/player/run_tests.js`): story moments rebuilt independently in JavaScript and equal to the CSV; every caption on every frame; leader changes on exactly the audited dates; final table = `career_totals.csv` ranks 1–20 and `topten_at_freeze.csv`; smooth pacing, reading time and holds. (7) **Audio:** none. The pilot was video only (the renderer encodes no audio track); the film keeps that, and the render workflow now fails if either file has an audio stream. No music has been chosen or licensed. (8) **Render:** `.github/workflows/render_pilot.yml` now renders the config chosen when run by hand (default the film; the pilot stays selectable) and runs on a push of the workflow file to this session's branch; release tag `rtt-002-film-<commit>-run<n>`, pre-release in the private repo only; no workflow artifact.

- **DEC-072 · 30 Sep 2026 · Owner (Luke), music; Claude working choices for the workflow.** Luke wants an exciting, fast, dramatic track from the **YouTube Audio Library**. The track is being chosen separately in Cowork; Claude does not choose music. Prepared: `.github/workflows/render_pilot.yml` takes optional inputs `music` (a file name in the private repo's `assets/rtt-002/`) and `music_sha256` (required with it; checked before use). `scripts/rtt_mix_music.sh` loops or cuts the track to the video's exact length and fades it out over the final table. It normalises in two passes (ffmpeg loudnorm, linear) to about **-16 LUFS integrated**, with true peak aimed at -1.5 dBTP, encodes AAC 320 kb/s 48 kHz, then measures again and **fails unless integrated is -16 ± 1 LUFS and true peak ≤ -1.0 dBTP**. It was tested in the container on synthetic tracks only (a short one that had to loop, a very quiet one and a peaky one): -16.0, -16.0 and -16.1 LUFS; true peak -7.3, -4.8 and -1.4 dBTP. The no-audio check is now conditional: with no music, every video must have no audio track; with music, every video must have exactly one. The workflow now renders a list of configs into one release. A push that changes the workflow file on this branch renders only the three 1080p speed clips (never the full film); runs started by hand default to the full film. Still needed before the music is used: the chosen track file uploaded to `assets/rtt-002/`, its SHA-256, and its licence terms from the YouTube Audio Library (attribution text, if the track requires it) recorded in `reference/rights_ledger.md`.

Recorded in `reference/rights_ledger.md`. The render checks this hash before use, like the logo and car (hash committed in `kits/rtt-002/music_rtt002.json`). - **DEC-074 · 30 Sep 2026 · Claude working choices (music loop and mix).** Beat tracking (librosa 0.11, in the container): about **103.4 BPM** (one beat every 0.5805 s); the steady beat starts at 9.45 s after an intro, there is a short break at about 104–108 s, and the track fades out from about 190 s. **Loop:** the track plays from 0 s (its intro). Instead of reaching its outro, it crossfades at **180.930 s (beat 293) back to 31.208 s (beat 37)**: exactly 256 beats = 64 bars, so every join lands on the same point of a phrase. That pair scores 1.504 of 2 on chroma plus timbre similarity; the best of any beat-aligned spacing scored 1.512, but its 272-beat body is not a whole number of 8-bar phrases. **Crossfade:** equal-power (quarter-sine), 8 beats = 4.644 s, centred on the two beats so they coincide. Checked on a film-length (563.6 s) test mix: joins at 180.9, 330.7 and 480.4 s; beat spacing across each join 0.580–0.604 s (no beat slip); level before, during and after each join within 0.4 dB; -16.0 LUFS integrated, true peak -4.0 dBTP; fades to silence over the last 10 s. Tempo, beats and loudness were measured; Claude cannot listen, so Luke should judge the joins by ear. **Mix:** `scripts/rtt_mix_music.sh` (loop, cut to length, fade out over the final table, two-pass loudnorm to -16 LUFS aimed at -1.5 dBTP, AAC 320 kb/s 48 kHz, re-measured; fails unless -16 ± 1 LUFS and true peak ≤ -1.0 dBTP). An ffmpeg `asplit` graph silently dropped audio on long loops (164.7 s out instead of 563.6 s), so each piece now reads its own input and the script fails if the loop comes out shorter than the video. **Workflow:** music on by default (input `music` true/false); file name, hash and loop points come from `kits/rtt-002/music_rtt002.json`; with music, every video must have exactly one audio track, without it none. The workflow's check and render steps were dry-run in the container on a short test passage with the real track and stand-in pictures: pass.

Counted as flash moments (distinct frames on which any highlight starts), the maximum is 3 per second, the same as in the approved pilot. The test now counts frames; the number of bars is still reported. The player and the pacing are unchanged.

- **DEC-076 · 30 Sep 2026 · Owner (Luke), speed.** Luke watched the three comparison clips (`rtt-002-speed-596f656-run3`) and chose **1.5× faster**: "it works really well with the music, the music is spot on". He was told that only the current pace keeps the film over 8 minutes (YouTube mid-roll eligibility) and chose 1.5× anyway. `config_rtt002_film.json`: `pacing.sec_per_event` 0.333 (exactly as the 1.5× clip); everything else unchanged: multipliers 1.4 / 1.0 / 0.8 on the top ten, 2.0 s record pauses, 10 s final table, no intro, no outro, no captions. Full film **11,852 frames = 6 min 35 s**. Music as in the 1.5× clip: "Ego Chall", hash-checked, looped on the beat (joins at about 181 s and 331 s), faded over the final table, -16 LUFS, true peak ≤ -1 dBTP. - **DEC-077 · 30 Sep 2026 · Owner (Luke), highlight at 1.5×.** Option 2: keep 1.5× and keep the winner highlight on every win, **accepting the 21 one-second windows in which highlights start on 4 different frames**. Luke was told this knowingly relaxes the DEC-030 no-strobing rule for this film, and that Claude in Cowork recommended option 1 (skip the 4th highlight). Condition: before rendering, measure the highlight against the WCAG 2.x general-flash and red-flash thresholds (area relative to the frame, luminance change) and record the result. Below the thresholds, the highlight does not count as a flash under WCAG: note that, make the 21 windows an owner-accepted exception in the RTT-002 film test only (the check stays for everything else), run the full suite and render. If it exceeds a threshold, do not render; report the numbers.
  - **Result (Claude Code, 30 Sep 2026; `reports/RTT-002_wcag_flash_check.md`, `tests/player/wcag_flash.js`):** on all 7,144 frames with a highlight lit, each frame was compared with the same frame drawn without the highlight. The largest luminance change is 0.275, so the highlight is a luminance transition (≥ 0.1), and red transitions occur (`#CC031B`). But the largest concurrent flash area in any 10° field (640 × 360 px, WCAG's 341 × 256 at 1024 × 768 scaled to the frame) is **11.4% for general flashes and 10.6% for red flashes, against the 25% limit**; the most in the whole frame is 2.1% of 1920 × 1080. So the highlight is **below the WCAG 2.x general- and red-flash thresholds** (condition 2) even in the 21 windows, and those do not count as failing flashes under WCAG. WCAG definitions as known to Claude (w3.org blocked from the container); the 10° scaling assumes full-screen viewing at a normal distance; not a medical or legal assessment. The RTT-002 film test case now carries an owner-accepted exception: up to 4 highlight starts per second, and it fails unless there are exactly 21 such windows. Every other case keeps the limit of 3.

- **DEC-119 · 2 Oct 2026 · Claude working choices (round 2), how Luke's changes are built — each open to Luke.**
  - **Counting (DEC-115):** each quarter's bars and labels move together on a straight line from the previous quarter's figures, in whole units, and land on the exact series.csv value on the quarter's last counting frame. While counting, "+" shows if either end is a lower bound; at the quarter end exactly the data's flag. The **order, the crown and the scoreboard follow the counted figures**, so an overtake happens on the frame where the numbers cross (the alternative — order jumping at the start of the quarter — showed e.g. PS2 above Game Boy while its number was still smaller). The date line names the quarter being counted. The one dip in the data (Super NES, -10,000 units in early 1997) never changes its "45.2m" label, so no label ever visibly goes backwards.
  - **Pauses after the overtake:** a crown-change quarter now counts at its normal pace and *then* pauses 2.0 s (round 1 replaced the beat with the pause, which put the overtake at the end of the pause). Callouts start on the frame the figures cross (crown changes, Game Boy past 100 million, DS passes Game Boy) and stay 3 s; the extra moments have no pause.
  - **15 rows:** names and values stay 33 px (6.7 pt on a phone) by narrowing the gaps between bars (14 → 8 px); pictures 81 × 49 px; the board starts 30 px lower again (220) to fit a second line under the title for the final-table footnote.
  - **Logos on the bars (DEC-116):** a light tile 78 × 39 px right of each picture, the logo fitted inside (as in the key). **Measured lettering** (each private SVG rasterised at 10×): PlayStation and Xbox are symbols (33 px tall = 6.7 pt) and read well; **"Nintendo" letters 7.2 px = 1.5 pt, "SEGA" 22 px = 4.5 pt, "ATARI" word 8.9 px = 1.8 pt** — all under the 5 pt phone floor, so **the text logos are not legible as text on a phone** (the red Nintendo pill, the blue SEGA shape and the Atari symbol may still be recognised by shape). Best alternative (DEC-120 proposal): the maker's name in plain type after the console name on the bar ("Game Gear · Sega", in the caption grey at the name size, 6.7 pt); it reads on any phone and needs no extra column.
  - **Tall consoles (DEC-114 (e)):** a portrait picture may now use up to 1.2× the box height (59 instead of 49 px), centred on its row; no picture changed. - **DEC-127 · 2 Oct 2026 · Claude working choices (round 3), how DEC-125 is built — each open to Luke.**
  - **Retirement quarter** = the first quarter end on or after the console's `fade_date` in `data/rtt-003/consoles.csv` (data unchanged). During that last quarter the row carries **"· retires"** after its value (so the tag shows while the date line reads the retirement quarter, e.g. "Atari 2600 … 25.0m+ · retires" at 31 Dec 1991); right after the quarter's count the bar fades over 0.5 s and leaves, and the rows below move up.
  - **Bars on screen** (measured at every quarter end of the film): **min 2, median 6, max 8** — the board never needs more than 8 rows; the 15-row sizing is kept (DEC-125 (d)).
  - **Record line:** gold, the full height of the board, at the largest all-time total of any console (retired included); its label "Record: <console> <value>" sits at the foot of the line, right-aligned to it; while the holder is on the board the crown is on its bar, after it retires the crown moves to the label. The scale always includes the record, so live bars visibly race towards it. A few consoles add a little after their retirement quarter (NES, SNES, DS +30,000; N64 +10,000; GBA +9,863 units in later quarters), so the all-time totals, the record and the scoreboard follow the data, and the "retires" figure is the total at retirement.
  - **Pace** judged on the live board (the bars actually on screen), 1.5 s per quarter × 1.4 / 1.0 / 0.8, 1985–88 at 0.75 s.
  - **Callout wording when the passed console has retired:** "PlayStation 2 becomes the best-selling console ever · passes Game Boy's 118.7m · estimated figures" and **"Nintendo DS passes Game Boy's 118.7m for second place all-time"**.
  - **Transition to the final table (1.6 s):** the record line and label fade in the first third; the live bars move to their all-time places; the retired consoles fade in (with the approved faded look) from 30% of the transition; the date line becomes "30 June 2026 · all-time top 15"; then the Switch line and the PS2 footnote; the table holds 10 s (DEC-070).

  - **Retirements set by where the figures stop** (the "data-gap" rule of DEC-125 (b)), for Luke: Master System (Mar 1993, 6.2m+), Mega Drive and Game Gear (Mar 1996), Saturn (Mar 1998), Xbox (Jun 2006, 24m+), PS2 and PSP (Mar 2012, Sony's last figures), PS3 (Mar 2017, 87.4m+), Xbox One (Sep 2021, last analyst figure). - **DEC-135 · 2 Oct 2026 · Claude working choices (round 4), how DEC-131/DEC-132 are built — each open to Luke.**
  - **Status in the data** (`series.csv` new column `status`; `consoles.csv` new columns `latest_figure_from`, `retired_from`, `status_basis`; build 1.1, check `bar_status_rules`). **Retired** from the first quarter end on or after a documented end date (`end_event_date`). **Latest figure** from the last quarter in which the bar added at least 10,000 units (the DEC-086 rule 2 threshold) when there is no documented end date by then. Live while on sale at 30 Jun 2026.
  - **"Latest figure" therefore covers two cases**, both listed in `reports/RTT-003_data_report.md`: (1) our figures run out — Master System (Sep 1993 to Mar 1996), Mega Drive, Saturn, PlayStation, Xbox, PS2, PSP, PS3, PS4, Xbox One; (2) the maker's figures continue but stop growing, with no documented end date in the data — NES, Game Boy, Super NES, Nintendo 64, Game Boy Advance, GameCube, Nintendo DS, Wii, 3DS, Wii U. Read literally, DEC-132 names only case (1); case (2) is labelled "latest figure" too, because "retired" needs a documented end date and an unlabelled static bar would read as live. Documented end dates for those consoles (a later data pass) would switch them to "retired". **Retired:** Atari 2600 (31 Dec 1991), Dreamcast (Mar 2001), Master System and Game Gear (Jun 1996), Xbox 360 (Jun 2016).
  - **On screen:** the status of a quarter applies from that quarter's quarter-end frame (when its count lands on the exact figure); the dim (bar and picture alpha 0.55, text 0.8) and the "· retired" / "· latest figure" label (after the value and any "· analyst estimate") fade in together over 0.6 s; a Master System bar switches its word from "latest figure" to "retired" without a second fade. A few bars still add under 10,000 units a quarter after they are labelled (NES, SNES, DS +30,000; N64 +10,000; GBA +9,863; Atari 2600 +2,017 by 1 Jan 1992) — the numbers follow the data.
  - **Record line:** at the all-time leader's figure (with every console on the board it is always the end of the top bar); drawn after the bars and broken wherever a value, label or crown crosses it; its label "Record: <console> <value>" now sits at the foot of the line on its RIGHT (round 3: left), in the space under the scoreboard, because the bottom rows' labels reach the foot of the line on the left. Crown on the leader's bar.
  - **VGChartz mapping:** each VGChartz monthly period ends with a reporting week 3–5 days after the calendar quarter end; that figure is placed at the quarter end (as Luke specified), so each Xbox Series point includes a few days after the quarter end.

  - **Rule 2 as built** (`scripts/build_rtt003_dataset.py` 1.2, check `bar_status_rules`; re-derived independently in `tests/player/run_tests_rtt003.js`): a bar not on sale stops in the last quarter it adds at least 10,000 units (as round 4). From then it is "retired" if the bar ends on a manufacturer's figure (grade A or B, not an analyst estimate) — the manufacturer's final total — or from the first quarter end on or after a documented end date; otherwise "latest figure". **Result:** latest figure on Mega Drive (from 31 Mar 1996), Master System (30 Sep 1993 to 31 Mar 1996, then retired from 30 Jun 1996) and **Game Gear for the single quarter end 31 Mar 1996** (its last Famitsu figure, two weeks before the Telecompaper report; retired from 30 Jun 1996) — one quarter more than Luke expected, kept because it is what the rule gives. Every other stopped bar reads "retired" (from its stop quarter): Atari 2600 Dec 1991; Saturn Mar 1998; SNES and Dreamcast Mar 2001; N64 Mar 2002; NES and Game Boy Mar 2003; PlayStation Mar 2005; Xbox Jun 2006; GameCube Mar 2008; GBA Dec 2009; PS2 and PSP Mar 2012; DS Mar 2014; Wii Mar 2016; Xbox 360 Jun 2016; PS3 and Wii U Mar 2017; 3DS Mar 2021; Xbox One Sep 2021 (documented end; its last figure is an analyst's); PS4 Mar 2022.

They move no bar; they also set the legacy `fade_date` column, which the round-5 configs do not use.

- **DEC-162 · 3 Oct 2026 · Claude working choice (refines DEC-157), CONFIRMED by Luke on 4 Oct 2026 (DEC-164).** A straight line joins two consecutive figures of one browser only if both come from the same source, or if they are the outgoing source's last date and the next source's first date (a hand-over). A single release that leaves a browser out does not break that source's line. Without this, Netscape would vanish for 18 months in 2004–05 because OneStat's 2004 releases do not list it. Effects: MacWeb never reaches a month end; Lynx and Mosaic stop in June 1997; Netscape stops in January 2007; Opera first appears in November 2004; Chrome first appears in January 2009.

- **DEC-170 · 4 Oct 2026 · Claude working choice, how smoothing options A, B and C (DEC-167) are drawn — for Luke to choose.** All drawn by the player at draw time (`rtt_timeline.js` `shareRace`); `series.csv` untouched.
  - **Gap stretches (A):** within one source, a monotone smooth curve (Fritsch–Carlson) through that source's published figures instead of straight lines. It passes through every published figure, never goes beyond the two figures either side, and comes to rest at a source's first and last figure.
  - **Hand-overs (A):** each bar blends gradually from the old source's line to the new source's line over **12 months centred on the hand-over** (the whole gap when it is longer), eased in and out. A blend of two published lines never goes outside the figures the two sources gave in that stretch (checked on every frame). The window never reaches into StatCounter's months: StatCounter values from January 2009 are always exact. **Effect:** EWS→StatMarket window July 2000 – July 2001, the largest difference from a published figure is Internet Explorer in December 2000, 80.3% drawn against 75.4% published; StatMarket→OneStat March 2002 – March 2003, largest 1.1 points (Netscape, May 2002); OneStat→W3Counter September 2006 – September 2007, largest Internet Explorer in January 2007, 80.9% against 85.81%. Outside the windows, smoothing moves an in-between month by at most 2.8 points (Mosaic, March 1994) and never moves a published figure. **The changes of first place are exactly as built** (Mosaic, Netscape June 1995, Internet Explorer October 1998, Chrome May 2012; checked on every frame of the whole film in A, B and C).
  - **B:** as A, plus every value before January 2009 reads as a whole-percent estimate, "~85%", or "<1%" below half a percent, so a smoothed bar never prints a precise number no source gave. From 2009 values are as published.
  - **C:** as B, plus the "New source" marker (DEC-172).
  - The two flagged hand-overs (DEC-168) are left as built in A, B and C unless Luke chooses to smooth them (DEC-171).
  - **A first attempt was replaced:** spreading each jump as a shift of the old source's line (old line + share of the jump) dipped below every published figure in places (Safari 2.35% in 2008 where the sources say 2.4–3.0%). The test caught it; the blend replaced it.
- **DEC-171 · 4 Oct 2026 · Claude finding, the two flagged stretches smoothed (brief item 2) — for Luke to decide.**
  - **GVU → EWS (16 Nov 1994 → 30 Apr 1996):** smoothed as one eased hand-over over the whole 531-day gap, Netscape passes Mosaic in **July 1995** instead of June 1995 (no figure behind either month; no date and no callout either way, DEC-165 (7)). Values are up to 6.3 points from the straight line (Netscape, February 1995) but no published figure moves.
  - **W3Counter → StatCounter (Dec 2008 → Jan 2009):** smoothed over the 12 months up to StatCounter's first month (January 2008 – January 2009), Opera passes Safari in **August 2008** instead of between December 2008 and January 2009; Internet Explorer in December 2008 is drawn 64.8% against W3Counter's 58.6%. StatCounter's months are unchanged.
  - Cowork's recommendation to Luke: smooth both, with no date shown for the Netscape takeover. Claude agrees for GVU → EWS (the gap has no figures at all, so a straight line is no more true than a curve). For W3Counter → StatCounter Claude leans the same way but notes that the smoothing moves eleven published W3Counter months to make the join invisible.

- **DEC-202 · 5 Oct 2026 · Claude proposal and working choices, the board-size option (answer 5, DEC-188) — for Luke to choose.** Player key `board.fit` {enabled, min_slots, sec} (`kits/rtt-002/player_rtt.html`, off for RTT-002 and RTT-003; `config_rtt001_approved_fit.json`). (1) **The rows are sized to the browsers on the board**, between **5 and 10 slots**: 2 browsers get the room of 5 (bars twice the top-10 thickness, 138 px against 69 px on the 1920 frame), never more, so a bar never becomes a slab; from 5 browsers up the bars fill the board, and from January 2009 (ten browsers) the board is exactly the top-10 board. (2) **Only the row height and the bar thickness change.** Names, values, logo tiles and the bars' left edge keep their top-10 size and place, so the text never shrinks below the phone floor (names 6.9 pt, values 7.3 pt; DEC-174) and nothing moves sideways. (3) **The motion:** the slot count is "browsers on the board" taken as the largest count within 1 s either side, then smoothed with a 2 s raised-cosine window: each resize eases in and out over about 2 s, the room opens before new browsers arrive and closes only after leaving ones have gone, so no bar is ever pushed off the board. Tested on every frame: at most 0.2 slots per frame and a change of speed of at most 0.012 slots per frame (no jump, no sudden start or stop). (4) **Where it shows:** 1994–2008, when the sources list 2–5 browsers; the biggest resize is January 2009, when StatCounter's figures begin and the board goes from 5 browsers to 10 (the clip). (5) **Claude's recommendation: bars sized to the board.** It removes most of the empty board in the 15 years before 2009 (45% of the film's running time) and the resize is slow; its drawback is that the bars change thickness, which viewers may notice once in January 2009.

- **DEC-212 · 5 Oct 2026 · Claude finding and fix, flashing in the RTT-001 film — for Luke to note.** **Finding:** the WCAG 2.x flash check of the whole film (`tests/player/wcag_flash_rtt003.js`, run in the Claude Code container before the render) **failed**: in August 1995, when Netscape passes Mosaic, the two thick striped bars (bars sized to the board, DEC-204) glide past each other and their diagonal "estimated" stripes, drawn relative to each bar, moved with them, so every pixel inside the bars flickered between stripe and gap: **90,544 px** flashing more than three times a second inside a 10° field, against WCAG's limit of **57,600 px** (25%). **Fix (Claude working choice, RTT-001 only):** the stripes are now anchored to the frame instead of the bar (player key `board.stripes: "screen"` in `config_rtt001_approved.json`); a bar gliding up or down moves under a still stripe pattern. Same angle, spacing, width and darkness, so stills look the same (side-by-side check of June 2005); only where each stripe starts along a bar differs. Frames 0–400 after the fix: **3,297 px** (from 90,544). RTT-002 and RTT-003 draw exactly as before (key absent). The full-film check runs again on the runner with the real logos before the render, and the render stops if it fails.

- **DEC-326 · 8 Oct 2026 · Owner (Luke), look-ahead method — owner exception to the evidence rules for the look-ahead only.** No company is dropped: Alibaba, Tencent, Baidu and CoreWeave stay in. Method: (1) where a company has said what it will spend, or which way, base its estimate on that statement; (2) otherwise take its recent actual capex (2024–2026) and apply the forecast uplift (growth) from the most authoritative published projections for 2027 onwards. Luke: "in the absence of hard figures, we need to make some assumptions"; "it is complete speculation. Everyone knows that"; "we're looking for the most authoritative sources". Extends DEC-306.

the source line under the title: 2026 "2026: company plans and latest figures · Alibaba: Citi estimate · ByteDance: press report", 2027–2030 "Race Through Time estimates · growth: FactSet consensus", plus "· 2030: least reliable year" at 2030 (in the date block it ran into the title); footers name each board's sources in one line. **Notes beside the values** (2026): Amazon "plan, as reported by Reuters"; Alphabet, Microsoft ("~$175bn"), Meta, CoreWeave "plan"; Oracle "year to May 2027, as reported by Reuters"; Alibaba "Citi estimate, year to Mar 2027"; Tencent and Baidu "actual to Jun 2026" (Tencent keeps "includes some intangibles", DEC-356); ByteDance "more than $29.3bn · press report", greyed with a dashed outline, a tenth row on the 2026 board only (no logo tile; never in the total, whose line says "sum of 9 companies · not ByteDance"). 2027–2028: Oracle "probably high" (where the file's notes say so), Alibaba "Citi estimate, year to Mar 2028 / 2029". **Moved to the explanation** (the 2026 board cannot carry them readably beside the total panel): Microsoft's and Meta's finance leases, Tencent's "aims to boost spending" note (DEC-343, DEC-356: its 2026 row already reaches the panel), Baidu's "no outlook", "unnamed sources" for ByteDance, CoreWeave's expected 2027 dip (DEC-342) and FactSet's full attribution. **Scale:** one scale for all five forward boards (the largest any of them needs to keep every label inside the frame and clear of the total panel and the cards), reached in the first half of the 2026 count; card labels now wrap inside the card. **Explanation** (after 2030, 14 s, phone-size text 36 px = 7.3 pt): four points - 2026, 2027–2030, least reliable, CoreWeave - and the full sources in its footer. **Steps after it:** the combined line (6 s), the peak timeline (8 s) and the final June 2026 table (10 s) as built in round 2. **Running time 5,475 frames = 3 min 2.5 s** without music (round 2: 2 min 50 s). - **DEC-502 · 8 Oct 2026 · Owner (Luke, 8 Oct 2026, Cowork chat, data questions; recorded in IQ-17).** (1) Use **every published month** that can be verified (all twelve months of 2025, the extra 2026 months and every other dated figure), not one per quarter. Anything still unresolved is listed for Luke (so ChatGPT May 2024 = 2.2 billion, not 2.5 billion). (3) DeepSeek's bar uses Similarweb's deepseek.com figures, which include the chat site chat.deepseek.com. (4) The research files go to the private repo `research/rtt-102/` (done by Cowork on 8 Oct).

Runner round R1 (`.github/workflows/rtt102_sources.yml`, https://github.com/marketmarathon/race-through-time/actions/runs/37790576703; no secrets, no artifacts, no cache) obeys robots.txt and does not fetch Similarweb's website profiles (free search limit not worked around) or the IPO PDF. - **DEC-506 · 8 Oct 2026 · Claude working choice (IQ-17), what counts as a point.** Only **monthly visits** count. Forecasts and projections (Similarweb's June 2024 and October 2023 projections), daily averages, unique visitors, shares and partial months never count, and no figure is multiplied, summed or repaired. Lower bounds ("more than 1 billion") count, flagged `bound = lower` (shown with "+", house style 3). Approximations ("about", "nearly") count, keeping their qualifier. Digiday's "Last month" (article dated 1 Dec 2023) is read as **November 2023**, as Cowork read it (V22).
- **DEC-507 · 8 Oct 2026 · Claude working choice (IQ-17), web addresses.** A press figure that names only the product (Digiday, The Decoder) counts for the bar's one address at that month. **For DeepSeek, which has two Similarweb addresses (deepseek.com and chat.deepseek.com), a press figure without an address does not count** (DEC-502 (3)): so The Decoder's 246.4 million for February 2026 and The Economic Times' 282 million for December 2025 are not used. Similarweb's own "DeepSeek" (April 2026 newsletter, 411 million) is read as deepseek.com, the address Similarweb's own table and newsletters use. In a monthly table the name and address are those in force at the end of the month (February 2024 reads "Gemini").
- **DEC-508 · 8 Oct 2026 · Claude working choice (IQ-17), applying the conflict rule (DEC-502 (2)).** The rule is applied to **VERIFIED, eligible figures only**. The same rule over every version (verified or not) shows what would change once Cowork checks the rest; such cases are marked PENDING. Versions that are the same number printed at a coarser precision, **rounded or cut** (e.g. "2.6 billion" and "2.595B", or Similarweb's "201M" for 201,518,109), **agree** and are not conflicts. Among agreeing versions the most reliable is used: Luke's three steps first, then exact over approximate over bound, then the most precise, then the earliest publication (e.g. ChatGPT May 2023 = 1.81 billion over 1.8 billion). A **leftover** uses no figure: the straight line runs across until Luke decides (questions 2 and 3 in the report).
- **DEC-509 · 8 Oct 2026 · Claude working choice (IQ-17), set-asides (`data/rtt-102/source/exclusions.csv`).** (1) Similarweb's November 2024 round-up, "November traffic hit ... 3.7 billion": probably October's figure under November's name (the same 3.7 billion and 115.9% growth as the October post it links to). (2) A "38K" table cell for Grok in July 2025 that its own page contradicts (prose 201M; 2025 table 201,518,109). (3) Two unit slips in a LinkedIn post ("6.2M" beside "topped 6 billion"; "1.2M" for Gemini). (4) **DataReportal's June 2024 figures** (ChatGPT "almost 2.9 billion", Gemini "more than 340 million"): published 31 Jul 2024, three days after the data-version change, but their "+14% month on month" fits the older May figure (2.5 billion), not the current one (2.2 billion), so their version is uncertain. (5) Rest of World's "1.7 billion by the end of November" 2023, which its own linked Similarweb chart gives for October.
- **DEC-510 · 8 Oct 2026 · Claude working choice (IQ-17), "visitors" wording.** Bard April 2023 (49.7 million) and May 2023 (142.6 million), and ChatGPT July 2023 ("about 1.5 billion"), are called "visitors" in Similarweb's text but are **used as visits**. Reasons: the May post states "142.6 million visits in May, up 187.2% from April", which is 49.7 million to 142.6 million, the same measure; a unique-visitor count of that size would imply over a billion visits, against 219.3 million visits in September 2023; and ChatGPT's unique visitors were about 140–210 million a month, not 1.5 billion.
- **DEC-511 · 8 Oct 2026 · Claude working choice (IQ-17), the series.** Published months show the used figure exactly. Months between two published months are a straight line by month index (whole visits, rounded half up), flagged `interpolated` with both end points. There are no values before a bar's first or after its last published month: no held values, no extrapolation. Flags on every row: `data_version` (older, current, or older->current on a line across 28 Jul 2024), `older_estimate` (yes if either end is older), `preliminary`, `rests_on_preliminary` and `bound`. Places are ranked on the bars that have a value that month.
- **DEC-512 · 8 Oct 2026 · Claude finding (IQ-17), what the data shows.** 233 figure rows from 52 publications; 183 VERIFIED (47 by the runner). **137 points are used** (125 Similarweb, 12 press), giving 203 bar-months (137 published, 66 on straight lines). ChatGPT leads every month. Second place: Bard/Gemini from March 2023; DeepSeek in January–April 2025 (277.9 million against 267.7 million in January); Gemini again from May 2025. Third place: Claude from November 2023 (an artefact, because Perplexity has no verified figure before March 2024); Perplexity from March 2024 (on a straight line); Gemini in January–April 2025; DeepSeek from May 2025; **Claude from March 2026** (613.7 million; DeepSeek on a straight line). **Coverage problem:** after June 2026 only ChatGPT has a verified figure (September 2026 profiles blocked by Similarweb's free search limit). Copilot has only three verified figures, Meta AI's last is December 2025 and Perplexity's is February 2026. Gemini's 433.5 million (March 2024, older version) to 247.3 million (September 2024, current version) may be partly or wholly Similarweb's re-estimate. Several publications hold months not yet extracted (report §6).

- **DEC-608 · 10 Oct 2026 · Claude finding (IQ-20), RTT-104 rights gate: THE BUILD IS STOPPED.** Read at source on the runner (10 Oct 2026, 06:02 UTC). **IPU** (https://www.ipu.org/terms-use, last update 24 April 2024, page SHA-256 73bc1afc…0d3d): "Use of the Inter-Parliamentary Union (IPU) websites, ipu.org, data.ipu.org and parliamentaryindicators.org, constitutes acceptance of these Terms of Use." … "All content on the IPU websites or extracts thereof may be displayed, reproduced, translated or adapted for research or personal use but not for sale or for use in conjunction with commercial purposes." … "The IPU’s open access policy applies to all the publications published by the IPU and is subject to the Creative Commons Attribution-NonCommercial-ShareAlike 4.0 International License." … "Any IPU datasets must also be made available to the public free of charge." … "Any use of datasets from data.ipu.org should be attributed as follows: “Inter-Parliamentary Union: dataset_name, month_year”". **World Bank** (WDI API metadata for SG.GEN.PARL.ZS, fetched file SHA-256 in the manifest): "License_Type": "CC BY-4.0", "License_URL": "https://datacatalog.worldbank.org/public-licenses#cc-by"; the licence page (https://datacatalog.worldbank.org/public-licenses): "The Creative Commons Attribution 4.0 International license allows users to copy, modify and distribute data in any format for any purpose, including commercial use." The two disagree: the World Bank labels the series CC BY 4.0, but its producer's own terms forbid commercial use of IPU content. The brief's condition (DEC-605) was met, so the build was stopped and the question put to Luke in the session; he chose to build the data and keep RTT-104 blocked (DEC-614). Not legal advice; Isle of Man law not checked. Still open for Luke: report question 1.
- **DEC-609 · 10 Oct 2026 · Claude finding (IQ-20), RTT-104 reference dates.** The UN SDG metadata for indicator 5.5.1(a) (IPU is the custodian; https://unstats.un.org/sdgs/metadata/files/Metadata-05-05-01a.pdf, read on the runner) gives the date each year's figure refers to: "2020 – Present As at 1 January"; "2013 – 2019 As at 1 February"; "2003, 2005 – 2007, 2009 - 2012 As at 31 January"; "2008 As at 29 January"; "2001, 2004 As at 30 January"; "2002 As at 4 February"; "1999 As at 5 February"; "1998, 2000 As at 25 January"; "1997 As at 1 January". Its definition: "The proportion of seats held by women in national parliaments, currently as of 1 January of reporting year". **Disagreement for 1998:** the WDI source note says "For the year of 1998, the data is as of August 10, 1998." (WDI metadata, date accessed 2026-03-29). Both are recorded; neither is chosen (no guessing). WDI's metadata also says: "Information is available in all countries where a national legislature exists and therefore does not include parliaments that have been dissolved or suspended for an indefinite period." The per-observation WDI footnotes are empty for every value.

- **DEC-621 · 10 Oct 2026 · Claude finding (IQ-20), RTT-104 a WDI year is not one fixed date.** IPU's archived monthly rankings (archive.ipu.org, runner run https://github.com/marketmarathon/race-through-time/actions/runs/38030108879) tested against WDI: WDI 1998 equals the 10 August 1998 ranking (as WDI's note says), WDI 2007, 2012 and 2017 equal the following January's ranking, and several values already include elections late in the WDI year (Rwanda September 2003 and 2013, Mexico July 2018, UAE October 2019), while others equal an earlier ranking (Kuwait 2013 and 2018, Sweden 2019). The UN SDG metadata's dates ("As at 1 January" from 2020 and so on) therefore do not describe WDI years. Inference from the comparison, not a quote. Recommendation (report question 2): show the year only. Detail: `data/rtt-104/source/reference_date_findings.md`.

- **DEC-630 · 10 Oct 2026 · Claude working choices under DEC-417 (IQ-22), design round 1 details (all shown to Luke as part of options a–i, nothing approved).** (a) Each board in one colour (teal for "Highest", amber for "Lowest") rather than one colour per country: 20 bars plus the 0% group would need 21 colours at least CIEDE2000 18 apart, and the flag already identifies each country; the board heading says which board is which. (b) Fixed scales, no gliding axis, so a bar's length always means the same share. (c) Layout A puts the name above each bar (a half-width board has no room for a name column at a phone-readable size); names, values and the 0% group at 30 px = 6.1 pt on a phone. (d) Rows move by their ranks 1–20, so a country comes up from below the board and goes down out of it; values count between the two years' figures and land exactly on the data's one-decimal text (house style 3). (e) A leave note shows for 1.8 s at the end of the last year with a figure (year label still that year, the note dated), then the move to the next year. (f) The world panel sits over rows 7–10 of the bottom board, from 0 to 30%, the line drawn to the current year with its value; it fits only with the shared scale. (g) Flags from flag-icons 7.5.0 (MIT), as RTT-002's approved flags (DEC-035), rather than Wikimedia Commons as the brief suggested: the same national flags, already in the rights ledger, no new download; every file's SHA-256 in `kits/rtt-104/flags_sha256.txt`. (h) The closing card adds a subtitle "Countries of 4 million people or more with no figure for 2025" (question). (i) Lead-in 1.5 s on 1997; final table 5 s (DEC-569); closing card 6 s.

- **DEC-654 · 10 Oct 2026 · Cowork's reading under DEC-417 (recorded by Claude Code; as approved for RTT-102 in DEC-562), what V2 is.** (1) Smoother: continuous eased motion with no stop-and-hold per year: a monotone curve (PCHIP, Fritsch–Carlson, as RTT-102) through every year's value as knots, so bars and rank changes glide from one year into the next and an election jump is spread across the whole year; every year's landing frame still equals `boards.csv` exactly; between landings the value labels show the interpolated value at one decimal, never beyond the next published value, never below 0; the year label changes when that year lands; the leave notes (Haiti, Kuwait, Pakistan, Egypt) and the 0% group keep their approved look, timed to their year's landing, with the same 1.8 s note hold. (2) Slower: 3 s per year (about 1:45); the music as before (no loop, 0.705 s start, 5 s fade). (3) Whole-film WCAG and film checks again; a new private pre-release.
- **DEC-655 · 10 Oct 2026 · Claude working choices (IQ-22d), how V2 is built.** `player_rtt104.html` gains `motion: "smooth"` (V1 unchanged and still selectable): each year is a knot; for every country ever on a board in the window, its rank on that board (capped at 23 = below the board) and its shown value are joined year to year by monotone cubic curves with Fritsch–Carlson slopes (harmonic mean of neighbouring changes, zero where the change reverses), so between two knots a curve never goes beyond either knot; the slope is zero at the first and last year and at the year where the board pauses for a leave note (the motion starts and stops at rest there) and next to a missing year; each move fills the whole 3 s year (no hold); a landing frame is drawn by V1's exact path (the data's own one-decimal text). The year label rolls in during the last 0.5 s before its year lands; the 0% row changes in the last 30% of the year; the world line grows continuously. A leaving country keeps the approved look B (greys with its note for 1.8 s at its last year's landing, then shrinks away in place while the others glide). The player input now carries every rank of each board (not just ranks 1–20), so a country entering the board glides up from its real rank; V1's path still uses ranks 1–20 only, so V1 draws as before (round-1 still checks unchanged, PASS). Config `kits/rtt-104/config_rtt104_film_v2.json` (= film config + smooth + 3 s per year): **3,111 frames = 1:43.7**. Film workflow switched to V2 (`FILM` config_rtt104_film_v2.json, files `rtt104_film_v2_<sha>_…`, release tag `rtt-104-film-v2-…`); V1 config kept. Seen in mid-year frames: where several rows swap places in one year they now pass through each other more slowly than in V1 (each swap spread across the year), as the brief's method implies. `kits/rtt-104/youtube_times.md` rewritten for V2's times.
- **DEC-656 · 10 Oct 2026 · Claude record (IQ-22d), V2 checks.** `tests/rtt104/film_check_rtt104.js` extended: besides every year's landing frame, every leave note and the closing card (34 shots, PASS), it now checks **every frame between landings** (2,492 frames): each value label lies between its two years' published figures (one-decimal rounding allowed) and is never below 0, and the year label is one of the two years: PASS. Whole-film WCAG flash check on V2 (3,111 frames): **PASS**, the largest too-often-flashing area 29,881 px of 57,600 allowed (frame 623; V1 51,183 px: the slower, smoother motion flashes less); red at most 826 px (`tests/rtt104/WCAG_FLASH_FILM_V2.md`). Data tests 23/23; round-1 still checks PASS.
